\end{note}








\begin{thm}
Let $[a,b]$ be a closed bounded interval, and let $f$ be defined real-valued function on $[a,b]$ which is continuous and bounded. Then 
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i).] If $P^*$ is a refinement of a partition $P$, we have $U(P^*, f)\leq U(P,f)$ and  $L(P^*,f)\geq L(P,f)$

\item[(ii).] For any two partitions $P_1$ and $P_2$, we have $L(P_1, f)\leq U(P_2,f)$
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}




\begin{proof}
To prove (i) it suffices to assume that $P^*$ contains exactly one more point than $P$, say the point $c$. Let $c$ be in the $j^{\text{th}}$ subinterval of $P$. Then 
$$U(P^*, f) = \sum^n_{\substack{k = 1\\
                              k\neq j}}M_k(f)\, \Delta x_k + M'\, (c - x_{k - 1}) + M''(x_k - c)$$
where $M'$ and $M''$ denote the supremum of $f$ in $[x_{k - 1}, c]$ and $[c,x_k]$ respectively. But since $M'\leq M_i(f)$ and $M'' \leq M_i(f)$ we have $U(P^*, f) \leq U(P,f)$.

The inequality for the lower sum is proved in a similar way.

To  prove (ii) Let $P= P_1\cup P_2$. Then we have 
$$L(P_1,f) \leq L(P,f) \leq U(P,f) \leq U(P_2,f)$$
$[$the lower sum of the refinement can not exceed that of the partition$]$.









\begin{remark}
If
\end{remark}
\begin{align*}
M & = \sup \{f(x):\, x\in [a,b]\}\\
m & = \inf \{f(x):\, x\in [a,b]\}
\end{align*}
and $P_1$ and $P_2$ are two partitions of $[a,b]$, then $m(b-a)\leq L(P_1,f) \leq U(P_2,f)\leq M(b-a)$.
\end{proof}





\begin{defn}
Let $f$ be a bounded function on a closed and bounded interval $[a,b]$. Then the upper Riemann integral of $f$ over $[a,b]$ is defined as 
$$\overline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx = \inf \{U(P,f):\, P\in \rho [a,b]\}$$
The lower Riemann integral of $f$ over $[a,b]$ is similarly defined to be
$$\underline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx = \sup \{U(P,f):\, P\in \rho [a,b]\}$$
That is, inf and sup taken over all possible partitions $P$ of $[a,b]$.


For simplicity, we shall denote the upper Riemann integral of $f$ in $[a,b]$ by $\overline{\int^b_a} f$ and the lower Riemann integral by $\underline{\int^b_a} f$ .
\end{defn}







\begin{thm}
Let $f$ be a bounded function on a closed and bounded interval $[a,b]$. Then $$\displaystyle{\underline{\int^b_a} f\, \leq \, \overline{\int^b_a} f}$$
\end{thm}



\begin{proof}
Let $\varepsilon > 0$ be given. Then there is a partition $P_1$ such that 
$$U(P_1,f ) < \overline{\int^b_a} f + \varepsilon$$
It follows that $\overline{\int^b_a} f + \varepsilon$ is an upper bound to all lower sum $L(P_1,f)$. Hence 
$$\underline{\int^b_a} f \leq \overline{\int^b_a} f + \varepsilon$$
since $\varepsilon$ is arbitrary, this implies that $$\displaystyle{\underline{\int^b_a} f \leq \overline{\int^b_a} f}$$
It is possible to have a strict inequality $$\displaystyle{\underline{\int^b_a} f < \overline{\int^b_a} f}.$$
\end{proof}





\begin{exa}
Let $f$ be defined on $[0,1]$ as follows
\[f(x)= 
\begin{cases}
1 & \text{if}\quad  x\quad \text{is rational}\\
0 & \text{if}\quad  x \quad  \text{is irrational}\\
\end{cases}
\]
Then for every partition $P$ of $[0,1]$ we have $M_k(f) = 1$ and $m_k(f) = 0$ since every subinterval contains both rational and irrational numbers. Therefore $U(P,f) = 1$ and $L(P,f) = 0$ for all $P$. It follows that on $[a,b] = [0,1]$
$$\overline{\int^b_a} f \, dx = 1\, , \,\underline{\int^b_a} f\, dx = 0.$$
\end{exa}












\begin{remark}
If $a< c < b$, then
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $\overline{\int^b_a} f \, = \, \overline{\int^c_a} f \, + \, \overline{\int^b_c} f$,
\item[(ii)] $\underline{\int^b_a} f \, = \, \underline{\int^c_a} f \, + \, \underline{\int^b_c} f$
\end{enumerate}



However, some properties of upper and lower integrals differ from those of integrals 
$$\overline{\int^b_a} (f + g) \leq \overline{\int^b_a} f \, + \, \overline{\int^b_a} g\qquad \text{and}\qquad \underline{\int^b_a} (f + g) \geq \underline{\int^b_a} f \, + \, \underline{\int^b_a} g.$$
\end{remark}






\begin{defn}
Let $f$ be a bounded function on the closed bounded interval $[a,b]$. Then $f$ is said to be Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$ if
$$\underline{\int^b_a} f\, dx \, = \, \overline{\int^b_a} f\, dx.$$


The common value of the upper and lower integrals is denoted by $\int^b_a f$ or $\int^b_a f(x)\,dx$ and is called the Riemann integral of $f$ with respect to $x$ in $[a,b]$.
\end{defn}





\begin{exa}
Every constant function $f(x) = C$ is Riemann integrable on any bounded interval.
\end{exa}


\begin{proof}
Let $P:\, a = < x_0 < x_i < \dots < x_k\,\dots \, x_{n - 1} < x_n = b$ be any partition, then 
\begin{align*}
U(P,f) & = \sum^n_{k = 1} M_k(f)\, \Delta x_k = \sum^n_{k = 1} C\, \Delta x_k\\
& = C\, (b - a)
\end{align*}

\begin{align*}
L(P,f) & = \sum^n_{k = 1} m_k (f) \, \Delta x_k = \sum^n_{k = 1} C\, \Delta x_k\\
& = C\, (b - a)
\end{align*}
Since the partition $P$ was arbitrary, we have $U(P,f) =  L(P,f) = C\, (b- a)$ for every partition $P$ of $[a,b]$. Thus
$$\overline{\int^b_a} f = C\, (b - a) = \underline{\int^b_a} f.$$


Therefore, $f$ is Riemann integrable and $$\displaystyle{\int^b_a f(x)\, dx = C\, (b - a)}.$$
\end{proof}







\begin{exa}
Let $f(x)  = x^2$ be a function defined on $[0,1]$. For each $n$, let $P_n$ be the the partition $\left\{0,\, \frac{1}{n},\, \frac{2}{n}, \, \dots \, \frac{n - 1}{n}, \, \frac{n}{n}\right\}$ of, the closed bounded interval $[0,1]$. Compute $\lim_{n\rightarrow \infty}\, U(P_n,f)$ and $\lim_{n \rightarrow \infty}\, L(P_n,f)$.
\end{exa}






\begin{solution}
The component intervals of the partition $P_n$ are $I_1 = [0, \frac{1}{n}]$, $I_2 = [\frac{1}{n}, \frac{2}{n}]$, \dots, $I_n = [\frac{n - 1}{n}, \frac{n}{n}]$, so that $\Delta x_k = \frac{1}{n}$ for $k = 1, 2, \dots, n$, and
\begin{gather*}
M_1(f) = \frac{1}{n^2}, \quad M_2(f)= \left(\frac{2}{n}\right)^2, \quad \dots, \\
M_k(f) = \left(\frac{k}{n}\right)^2, \quad \dots, \quad M_n(f) = \left(\frac{n}{n}\right)^2 .
\end{gather*}
Also
\begin{gather*}
m_1(f) = 0, \quad m_2(f)= \left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^2, \quad m_3(f) = \left(\frac{2}{n}\right)^2, \quad \dots, \\
m_k (f) = \left(\frac{k - 1}{n}\right)^2, \quad \dots, \quad m_n(f) = \left(\frac{n - 1}{n}\right)^2 .
\end{gather*}






\begin{align*}
U(P_n,f) & = \sum^n_{k = 1} M_k(f)\, \Delta x_k = \sum^n_{k = 1}\left(\frac{k}{n}\right)^2 \cdot \frac{1}{n}\\
 & = \frac{1}{n^2} \cdot \frac{1}{n} + \left(\frac{2}{n}\right)^2\cdot\frac{1}{n} + \cdots + \left(\frac{n}{n}\right)^2 \cdot \frac{1}{n}\\
 & = \dfrac{1}{n^3}\, (1 + 2^2 + 3^2 + \dots + n^2)\\
 & = \frac{1}{n^3}\, \left(\dfrac{n\,(n + 1)\, (2n + 1)}{6}\right)\\
 & = \dfrac{n\, (n+1)\, (2n + 1)}{6\, n^3}\\
\therefore\quad  U(P_n,f) & = \dfrac{\dfrac{n}{n}\, \left(\dfrac{n + 1}{n}\right)\, \left(\dfrac{2n + 1}{n}\right)}{6}
\end{align*}

Taking the limit as $n \longrightarrow \infty$
$$\lim_{n\rightarrow \infty}\,U(P_n,f) = \lim_{n\rightarrow \infty}\,\dfrac{\dfrac{n}{n}\, \left(\dfrac{n + 1}{n}\right)\, \left(\dfrac{2n + 1}{n}\right)}{6} = \frac{1\, (2)}{6}  = \frac{1}{3}$$



Similarly,
\begin{align*}
L(P_n,f) & = \sum^n_{k = 1}m_k(f)\, \Delta x_k = \sum^n_{k = 1}\left(\frac{k - 1}{n}\right)^2 \cdot \frac{1}{n}\\
& = \frac{0}{n^2} \cdot \frac{1}{n} + \frac{1}{n^2} \cdot \frac{1}{n} + \cdots + \left(\frac{n - 1}{n}\right)^2 \cdot \frac{1}{n}\\
& = \frac{1}{n^3}\,\,( 0 + 1^2 + 2^2 + 3^2  + \,\dots \, + (n - 1)^2)\\
& = \frac{1}{n^3}\,\,(1^2 + 2^2 + 3^2 + \,\dots \, + \, (n - 1)^2)\\
& = \frac{1}{n^3}\, \,\left(\dfrac{(n - 1)\, n\, (2n - 1)}{6}\right)\\
& = \frac{(n - 1) \, (2n - 1)}{6\, n^2} \longrightarrow \frac{2}{6} = \frac13 \quad\text{as } n \rightarrow \infty .
\end{align*}
Since $L(P_n, f) \leq \underline{\int^1_0} f \leq \overline{\int^1_0} f \leq U(P_n, f)$ and both bounds tend to $\frac13$, $f$ is integrable and $\int^1_0 x^2\, dx = \frac13$.
\end{solution}











\begin{thm}
Let $f$ be a bounded function defined on a closed and bounded interval $[a,b]$. Then $f$ is Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$ if and only if for every $\varepsilon >0$, there is a partition $P$ of $[a,b]$ such that $U(P, f) - L(P,f) < \varepsilon$.
\end{thm}




\begin{proof}
We have already seen that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lowup}
\underline{\int^b_a} f(x)\, dx \, \leq \, \overline{\int^b_a} f(x)\, dx .
\end{equation}
Suppose now that for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is a partition $P$ for which $U(P,f) - L(P, f) < \varepsilon$. Since
$$\overline{\int^b_a} f(x) \, dx \leq U(P,f) \qquad\text{and}\qquad \underline{\int^b_a} f(x)\,dx \geq L(P,f),$$
we have
$$\overline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx - \underline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx < \varepsilon .$$
Since $\varepsilon  > 0$ is arbitrary, we get
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uplow}
\overline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx \, \leq \, \underline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx .
\end{equation}
Hence from \eqref{eq:lowup} and \eqref{eq:uplow} we get
$$\underline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx\, = \, \overline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx$$
So that $f$ is Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$.

Conversely, suppose that $f$ is Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$. Then we have
\begin{align*}
\overline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx \, & = \inf_P\, U(P_1,f)\\
& = \sup_P\, L(P_2,f)\\
& = \underline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx
\end{align*}
Let $\varepsilon >  0$ be given. Then we can choose partitions $P_1$ and $P_2$ such that
$$U(P_1,f) < \overline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx + \frac{\varepsilon}{2} \qquad\text{and}\qquad L(P_2,f) > \underline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx - \frac{\varepsilon}{2}.$$ Since $f$ is Riemann integrable, we have
$$U(P_1,f) - \frac{\varepsilon}{2}< \overline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx  = \underline{\int^b_a}f(x)\, dx < L(P_2,f) + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$$
That is $U(P_1,f) - \frac{\varepsilon}{2} < L(P_2,f) + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$

Now, considering the common partition of $P_1$ and $P_2$ we have 
$$U(P_1\cup P_2,f) - \frac{\varepsilon}{2} < L(P_1\cup P_2,f) + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$$
Let $P_1\cup P_2$ be a single partition $P$. Then
$$U(P,f) - \frac{\varepsilon}{2}  < L(P,f) + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$$
This gives $U(P,f) - L(P,f) < \varepsilon$.
\end{proof}






\begin{thm}
Every continuous function on the interval $[a,b]$ is Riemann integrable.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Recall that a continuous function on a closed bounded interval is uniformly continuous there.
Suppose that $f$ is continuous on $[a,b]$ and let $\varepsilon > 0$ be given, we show that corresponding to this $\varepsilon > 0$, there is partition $P$ such that $U(P,f) - L(P,f) < \varepsilon$ and then by the above Theorem shall conclude that $f$ is Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$.

Now by uniform continuity of $f$ on $[a,b]$, there is a $\delta > 0$ such that if $x, \, y\in [a,b]$ with $|x - y| < \delta$ then $|f(x) - f(y)| < \frac{\varepsilon}{b - a}$.

Let $P$ be any partition of $[a,b]$ with $\|P\| < \delta$. By the property of continuous functions, on a closed bounded interval $[x_{k  -1}, x_k]$, there exists points $x_k'$ and $x_k''$ such that  $f(x_k') = M_k$ and $f(x_k'')  = m_k$. Now
\begin{align*}
|x_k' - x_k''| \, & \leq |x_k - x_{k - 1}| = |\Delta x_k|\\
& < \|P\|\\
& < \delta
\end{align*}
Hence $M_k - m_k = |f(x_k') - f(x''_k)| < \frac{\varepsilon}{b - a}$ for $k = 1,\, 2,\, \dots \, n$. Thus
\begin{align*}
U(P,f) - L(P,f) \, & = \sum^n_{k = 1}[M_k(f) - m_k(f)]\,|\Delta x_k|\\
& = \sum^n_{k = 1}|f(x'_k) - f(x''_k)|\, |\Delta x_k|\\
& < \dfrac{\varepsilon}{b - a}\, \sum^n_{k = 1}|\Delta x_k|\\ 
& = \dfrac{\varepsilon}{b - a}\, (b - a) = \varepsilon
\end{align*}
Thus $U(P,f) - L(P,f) < \varepsilon$.

Hence from the above theorem, $f$ is Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{proof}










\begin{thm}
A monotone function $f$ on $[a,b]$ is Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{thm}


\begin{proof}
If $f$ is a constant function on $[a,b]$ then it is Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$ by earlier example.
Assume that $f$ is monotone increasing function on $[a,b]$ and $f(a) < f(b)$.
Let $\varepsilon > 0$ be given and let $P$ be a partition on $[a,b]$ with $\|P\| = \frac{\varepsilon}{f(b) - f(a)}$. Since $f$ is increasing on $[a,b]$ we have on $[x_{k - 1}, x_k],\quad  M_k(f) =f(x_k)$ and $m_k(f) = f(x_{k-1})$ for $k = 1,\, 2,\, \dots \, n$. Hence
\begin{align*}
U(P,f) - L(P,f) \, & = \sum^n_{k = 1} M_k(f)\, \Delta x_k - \sum^n_{k = 1} m_k(f)\, \Delta x_k\\
& = \sum^n_{k  = 1} [M_k(f) - m_k(f)]\, \Delta x_k \\
& = \sum^n_{k  = 1} [f(x_k) - f(x_{k - 1})]\, \Delta x_k\\
& < \frac{\varepsilon}{f(b) - f(a)}\, \sum^n_{k  = 1} [f(x_k) - f(x_{k - 1})]\\
& = \dfrac{\varepsilon}{f(b) - f(a)}\, (f(x_n)-f(x_0))\\
& = \dfrac{\varepsilon}{f(b) - f(a)}\, (f(b)  - f(a)) = \varepsilon
\end{align*}
That is $U(P,f) - L(P,f) < \varepsilon$ and so by earlier Theorem, $f$ is Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$.\end{proof}

$*$ Prove for the decreasing function.






\begin{exa}
Let $f$ be a continuous function on $[0,1]$ and let $\sigma_n = \left\{0\, ,\, \frac{1}{n}\, ,\, \frac{2}{n}\, , \, \dots \, \frac{n}{n}\right\}$ be a partition of $[0,1]$. Let $x^*_k$ be any point in the interval $\left[\frac{k - 1}{n}\, , \, \frac{k}{n}\right]$
\begin{enumerate}
\item[i).] Prove that $L(\sigma_n, f) \leq \frac{1}{n}\, \sum_{k =1}^n f(x^*_k) \leq U(\sigma_n,f)$
\item[ii).] hence show that $\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\, \sum^n_{k  = 1} f(x^*_k) = \int^1_0f(x)\, dx$
\end{enumerate}
\end{exa}





\begin{proof}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[i).] To get the first inequality, $L(\sigma_n, f) \leq \frac{1}{n}\, \sum^n_{k = 1} f(x^*_k)$, note that $m_k(f)\leq f(x^*_k)$ so that 
\begin{align*}
\sum^n_{k  = 1} m_k(f)\, \Delta x_k & \leq \sum^n_{k  = 1}f(x^*_k)\, \Delta x_k\\
\implies\quad  \sum^n_{k  = 1}m_k(f)\, \Delta x_k &  \leq \dfrac{1}{n}\, \sum^n_{k  = 1}f(x^*_k)\\
\implies\quad  L(\sigma_n, f) & \leq \dfrac{1}{n}\,\sum^n_{k  = 1} f(x^*_k)
\end{align*}
The second inequality, $\frac{1}{n} \sum^n_{k  = 1}f(x^*_k) \leq U(\sigma_n , f)$, is obtained in a similar fashion since $f(x^*_k) \leq M_k(f)$ for each $k$.

Hence $L(\sigma_n, f) \leq \frac{1}{n}\, \sum^n_{k  = 1} f(x^*_k) \leq U(\sigma_n, f)$ as required.


\item[ii).] Since $f$ is continuous on $[0,1]$ it is bounded on $[0,1]$, given a $\varepsilon > 0$ there exits a partition such that $U(\sigma_n, f) - L(\sigma_n,f)< \varepsilon$. But
$$L(\sigma_n,f)\leq \int^1_0f(x)\, dx \implies U(\sigma_n,f) - \int^1_0f(x)\, dx < \varepsilon$$
for large $n$:
$$U(\sigma_n, f) - L(\sigma_n,f) < \varepsilon$$
$$U(\sigma_n,f) - \int^1_0f(x)\, dx \, + \, \int^1_0f(x)\, dx - L(\sigma_n,f) < \varepsilon$$
But $\int^1_0f(x)\, dx \, -\, L(\sigma_n,f) >0$.   Therefore $U(\sigma_n,f) \, - \, \int^1_0f(x)\, dx < \varepsilon$

Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limU}
\lim_{n\rightarrow \infty}\, U(\sigma_n, f) = \int^1_0 f(x)\, dx .
\end{equation}


Similarly $\int^1_0f(x)\, dx  - L(\sigma_n, f) < \varepsilon$ so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limL}
\lim_{n\rightarrow \infty} L(\sigma_n,f) = \int^1_0f(x)\, dx .
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{eq:limU} and \eqref{eq:limL} with $L(\sigma_n,f) \leq \frac{1}{n}\sum^n_{k=1} f(x^*_k) \leq U(\sigma_n,f)$ gives $$\int^1_0f(x)\, dx \leq \lim_{n\rightarrow \infty}\, \dfrac{1}{n}\, \sum^n_{k = 1} f(x^*_k) \leq \int^1_0f(x)\, dx$$

Hence $\lim_{n\rightarrow \infty} \, \frac{1}{n}\, \sum^n_{k = 1}f(x^*_k)=\int^1_0f(x)\, dx$
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}









\begin{exa}
Let $f:\, [0,1]\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ be defined by 
\[f(x) = 
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{\sin x}{x} & \text{if}\quad  x\neq 0\\
1 & \text{if}\quad  x = 0\\
\end{cases}
\]
Prove that $f$ is Riemann integrable on $[0,1]$.
\end{exa}


\begin{proof}
Since $\frac{\sin x}{x}$ is continuous for all $x\neq 0$ and $\lim_{x\rightarrow 0} \frac{\sin x}{x} = 1 = f(0),$ we see that $f$ is continuous on a closed bounded interval $[0,1]$. Therefore, $f$ is Riemann integrable on $[0,1]$.
\end{proof}






\begin{exa}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[i).] Consider the functions $f$ defined on the interval $[0,1]$ by
\begin{center}
$f(x)  = 1 $ when $x$ is rational\\
$f(x) = 0$ when $x$ is irrational
\end{center} 
Then $f$ is a bounded function on $[0,1]$. However, $f$ is not Riemann integrable on $[0,1]$.


\item[ii).] We give an example of a function which is not continuous but Riemann integrable.

Let $f(x) = 1$ if $x\in \left[0\, ,\, \frac{1}{2}\right]$ and $f(x) = 0$ if $x\in \left(\frac{1}{2}\, ,\, 1\right]$. Then $f$ is not continuous at $x=\frac{1}{2}$, since left limit is not equal to right limit. To see that $f$ is Riemann integrable consider any partition $P$ of $[0,1]$ given by $$P:\, 0 = x_0 < x_1 < \dots \, < x_{k - 1} < x_k < \, \dots \, < x_n = 1$$ 
Suppose that $\frac{1}{2}\in [x_{k - 1},x_k)$ in the partition. Then in $[x_{k - 1}, x_k),\quad  M_k(f) = 1$ and $m_k(f) = 0$. We also have $M_i(f) = m_i(f) = 1$ in $[x_{i - 1}, x_i]$ for $i = 1,\, 2,\, \dots \, k-1$ and $M_i(f) = m_i(f) = 0$ for $i = k,\dots \, n$. Then
\begin{align*}
U(P,f) & = (x_1 - x_0) + \cdots + (x_k - x_{k - 1}) + 0 + \cdots + 0 = x_k\\
L(P,f) & = (x_1 - x_0) + \cdots + (x_{k-1} - x_{k -2}) + 0  + \cdots + 0=x_{k -1}
\end{align*}
It follows that $\overline{\int^1_0}f(x)\,dx = \inf\, U(P,f) = 1$
$$\underline{\int^1_0}f(x)\,dx = \sup\, L(P,f) = 1$$
Hence $\overline{\int^1_0}f(x)\,dx = 1 = \underline{\int^1_0}f(x)\,dx$ and $f$ is Riemann integrable on $[0,1]$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exa}









\begin{thm}
A bounded function on $[a,b]$ is Riemann integrable if on $[a,b]$ 
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i).] it has a finite set of points of discontinuity.
\item[(ii).] its set of points of discontinuity has a finite number of limit points.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}






\begin{proof}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i).] If $f$ is discontinuous on $[a,b]$ then  $$M = \sup\{f(x):\, x\in [a,b]\} \neq \inf \{f(x):\, x\in [a,b]\} = m$$
Let $\{\alpha_1,\, \alpha_2,\, \dots ,\, \alpha_p\}$ be the ordered set of $p$ points of discontinuity of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Let $\alpha_1\neq a$ and $\alpha_p \neq b$. For $\varepsilon > 0$, enclose these points of discontinuity in non-overlapping subintervals $[x_1', x''_1]\,,\, [x'_2,x_2'']\, ,\dots ,\, [x_p',x_p'']$ of $[a,b]$ such that their total length is less than $\frac{\varepsilon}{2\, (M - m)}$
$$\text{i.e}\qquad \sum^p_{k = 1}(x''_k - x'_k)\, < \, \dfrac{\varepsilon}{2\, (M - m)}$$
The function is then continuous on each of the $p + 1$ subintervals $[a,x_1']$, $[x''_1,x'_2]$, $[x''_2,x'_3]$, \dots, $[x''_p,b]$, so for $\varepsilon > 0$ there exist partitions $P_1$, $P_2$, \dots, $P_{p+1}$ of these subintervals such that
$$U(P_r,f) - L(P_r,f) < \dfrac{\varepsilon}{2\,(p+1)}\quad  r =1,\, 2,\, \dots ,\, p + 1$$
Then for the partition $P= \{a = x_0,\, x_1,\, \dots ,\, x_1',\, x_p',\, x_p'',\, \cdots\, x_n = b\}$ of $[a,b]$ we have
\begin{align*}
U(P,f) - L(P,f) & = \sum^p_{r=1} M_r'(f)\, (x''_r-x'_r) + \sum^{p+ 1}_{r=1}U(P_r,f) \\
& \qquad - \sum^p_{r=1}m'_r(f)\,(x''_r-x'_r) - \sum^{p + 1}_{r=1}L(P_r,f)\\
& = \sum^p_{r=1}(M'_r(f) - m'_r(f))\,(x''_r-x'_r) \\
& \qquad + \sum^{p + 1}_{r=1}(U(P_r,f) - L(P_r,f))
\end{align*}
where $M'_r(f) = \sup\{f(x):\, x\in [x'_r,x''_r]\}$ and $m'_r(f) = \inf\{f(x):\, x\in [x'_r,x''_r]\}$. Thus
\begin{align*}
U(P,f) - L(P,f) & < \sum^p_{r=1}(M -m)\, (x''_r-x'_r) + \dfrac{\varepsilon}{2\, (p + 1)}\, \cdot\, (p + 1)\\
& < (M-m) \, \dfrac{\varepsilon}{2(M -m)}\, + \, \frac{\varepsilon}{2} = \varepsilon
\end{align*}
Hence $f$ is Riemann integrable.

The case $\alpha_1 = a$ or $\alpha = b$ can easily be dealt with by slight modifications in the above arguments for $x'_1 = a$ or $x''_p = b$.


\item[(ii).] To prove the second part, let $\{\alpha_1, \alpha_2, \dots, \alpha_p\}$ be the ordered set of finite number of limit points of the set of discontinuities of $f$ on $[a,b]$. Let $\alpha_1\neq a$ or $\alpha_p\neq b$. For $\varepsilon >0$, enclose these limit points in $p$ non-overlapping subintervals $[x_1',x_1'']$, $[x'_2,x_2'']$, \dots, $[x_p',x_p'']$ whose total length is less than $\frac{\varepsilon}{2(M-m)}$,
$$\text{i.e}\qquad \sum^p_{k = 1}(x''_k - x'_k) \, < \, \dfrac{\varepsilon}{2\, (M-m)}$$
In each of the remaining $p + 1$ subintervals $[a,x_1']\, ,\, [x_1'',x'_2]\, , \, \dots \, [x''_p, b]$ of $[a,b]\quad  f$ has only a finite number of points of discontinuity so that each of these $p+1$ subintervals can be partitioned to $P_1,\, P_2,\, \dots \, P_{p+1}$ such that 
$$U(P_r,f) - L(P_r,f) < \dfrac{\varepsilon}{2(p + 1)}\quad  r = 1,\, 2,\, \dots \, p+1$$
Then for the partition $P$ as in (i) we get $U(P,f) - L(P,f) < \varepsilon$.
Hence $f$ is Riemann integrable.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}



















\subsection{Properties of the Riemann Integral}

We now consider Riemann integrable functions on a closed bounded interval $[a,b]$ and establish some of their properties.

We shall denote the set of all Riemann integrable functions on $[a,b]$ by $R[a,b]$.

That is , we shall write $f\in R[a,b]$ to mean that $f$ is Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$ or $f$ belongs to the class of Riemann integrable functions on $[a,b]$.







\begin{thm}
If $f\in R[a,b]$ and $C$ is any real number, then $C\, f\in R[a,b]$ and $$\int^b_aCf \, = \, C\, \int^b_af.$$
\end{thm}



\begin{proof}
Since $f\in R[a,b]$ then $\overline{\int^b_a}f\, = \,\underline{\int^b_a}f$ and $f$ is bounded on $[a,b]$. Now $|Cf| = |C|\,|f|$ which implies that $Cf$ is bounded on $[a,b]$.
Now, for $C = 0$, the result is obvious.

If $C>0$, let $P$ be a partition of $[a,b]$. Then
\begin{align*}
U(P,Cf) & = \sum^n_{k = 1}C\, M_k(f)\, \Delta x_k\\
& = C\, \sum^n_{k = 1} M_k(f)\, \Delta x_k\\
& = C\, U(P,f)
\end{align*}
Thus $\inf U(P,Cf)  = C \inf U(P, f)$, and also $\sup L(P, Cf) = C \sup L(P,f)$. Therefore
$$\overline{\int^b_a}Cf = \inf(U(P,Cf)) = C\, \inf U(P,f) = C\, \overline{\int^b_a}f$$
Since $f$ in Riemann integrable, we have that
\begin{align*}
C\,\overline{\int^b_a}f  = C\,\underline{\int^b_a}f  &  = C\, \sup L(P,f)
 = \sup L (P, Cf) = \underline{\int^b_a}Cf
\end{align*}




Thus $\overline{\int^b_a}Cf = \underline{\int^b_a}Cf$ and $Cf\in R[a,b]$.

Since $\overline{\int^b_a}Cf = C\,\underline{\int^b_a}f$ we have $\int^b_aCf \, = \, C\, \int^b_af$.


If $C< 0$, then $C\, M_k(f) = \inf\{C\,f(x):\, x\in [x_{k-1},x_k]\}$ and

$C\, m_k(f) = \sup\{C\,f(x):\, x\in [x_{k-1},x_k]\}$ and so

$\sup\{U(P,Cf):\, P\in P[a,b]\} = C\, \inf\{U(P,f):\, P\in P[a,b]\}$ and

$\inf\{L(P,Cf):\, P\in P[a,b]\} = C\, \sup \{L(P,f):\, P\in P[a,b]\}.$ Therefore
\begin{align*}
\overline{\int^b_a} Cf & = \inf\{L(P,Cf):\, P\in P[a,b]\}
                        = C\, \sup \{L(P,f):\, P\in P[a,b]\}\\
                       & = C\, \underline{\int^b_a}f = C\, \int^b_af = C\, \overline{\int^b_a}f\\
                       & = C\, \inf\{U(P,f):\, P\in P[a,b]\}
                         = \sup\{U(P,Cf):\, P\in P[a,b]\}\\
                       & = \underline{\int^b_a}Cf
\end{align*}
Hence again $Cf\in R[a,b]$ and $\int^b_a Cf\, = \, C\, \int^b_af$.
\end{proof}






\begin{thm}
If $f\in R[a,b]$ and $c\in [a,b]$ then $f\in R[a,c]$ and $f\in R[c,b]$ and conversely. In this case
$$\int^b_af \, = \,\int^c_af \, + \, \int^b_cf.$$
\end{thm}


\begin{proof}
For $c\in [a,b]$ we have that $f$ is bounded on $[a,c]$ and on $[c,b]$ if and only if it is bounded on $[a,b]$. Since $f\in R[a,b]$ given $\varepsilon >0$ there exists a partition $P$ such that $$U(P,f) - L(P,f) < \varepsilon$$
Let $P^* = P\cup \{c\}$ be a refinement of $P$ by adding a point, then $P^*\in [a,b]$ and 
$$U(P^*,f) - L(P^*,f)\leq U(P,f) - L(P,f) < \varepsilon$$
We break the partition $P^*$ on $[a,b]$ to $P_1$ on $[a,c]$ and $P_2$ on $[c,b]$. Then we have
\begin{align*}
U(P^*,f) - L(P^*,f) & = U(P_1,f) + U(P_2,f) - L(P_1,f) - L(P_2,f)\\
& = (U(P_1,f) - L(P_1,f)) + (U(P_2,f) - L(P_2,f))\\
& < \varepsilon
\end{align*}
It follows that $U(P_1,f) - L(P_1,f) < \varepsilon$ and $U(P_2,f) - L(P_2,f) < \varepsilon.$ Thus $f\in R[a,c]$ and $f\in R[c,b]$.

Now it can be shown that $U(P_1,f) + U(P_2,f) = U(P^*,f)$ implies that 
$$\inf\, U(P_1,f) \, + \, \inf\, U(P_2,f)\, = \, \inf\, U(P^*,f)$$
This then implies that $\int^c_af\, + \, \int^b_cf \, = \, \int^b_af.$


Conversely, in $c\in (a,b)$ and $f\in R[a,c]$ and $f\in R[c,d]$, then there exists partitions $P_2\in P[a,c]$ and $P_2\in P[c,b]$ such that $U(P_1,f) - L(P_1,f) < \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ and $U(P_2,f) - L(P_1,f)<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}$. Then for $P= P_1\cup P_2$ we have
\begin{align*}
U(P,f) - L(P,f) & = U(P_1,f) + U(P_2,f) - L(P_1,f) - L(P_2,f)\\
& = U(P_1,f) - L(P_1,f) + U(P_2,f) - L(P_2,f)\\
& < \varepsilon
\end{align*}
Hence $f\in R[a,b]$ and the required relation of integrals follow as before.
\end{proof}






\begin{coro}
If $f\in [a,b]$ and $[c,c]\subset [a,b]$ then $f\in R[c,d]$. That is if $f$ is Riemann integrable on a closed bounded interval, then it is Riemann integrable on every closed subinterval.
\end{coro}

\begin{proof}
Let $\varepsilon>0$. Since $f\in R[a,b]$, Riemann's criterion gives a partition
$P$ of $[a,b]$ with $U(f,P)-L(f,P)<\varepsilon$. Refine $P$ by adjoining the
points $c$ and $d$; refinement never increases the difference, so the refined
partition $P'$ also satisfies $U(f,P')-L(f,P')<\varepsilon$.

Let $Q$ be the part of $P'$ lying in $[c,d]$, a partition of $[c,d]$. Every term
of $U(f,Q)-L(f,Q)$ appears among the terms of $U(f,P')-L(f,P')$, and all terms
are non-negative, so
$$U(f,Q)-L(f,Q) \leq U(f,P')-L(f,P') < \varepsilon .$$
By Riemann's criterion again, $f\in R[c,d]$.
\end{proof}





\begin{thm}
Suppose that $f,\, g\in R[a,b]$, then $f + g\in R[a,b]$ and $$\int^b_a(f  + g) \, = \, \int^b_af \, + \, \int^b_ag$$
\end{thm}





\begin{proof}
Since $f,\, g\in R[a,b]$, then there functions are bounded on $[a,b]$. Therefore there exists on $M>0$ such that $|f(x)| \leq M$ and $|g(x)| \leq M$ for all $x\in [a,b]$. Thus $$|f(x) + g(x)| \leq |f(x)| + |g(x)|\leq 2M$$ for all $x\in [a,b]$. This shows that $f + g$ is bounded on $[a,b]$. 
Therefore $\overline{\int^b_a}(f + g)$ and $\underline{\int^b_a}(f + g)$ exists.

But if $A\subseteq [a,b]$ then we have $\{f(x) + g(x):\, x\in A\} \subset \{f(x_1) + g(x_2):\, x_1,\, x_2\in A\}$.  Thus for any partition $P$ of $[a,b]$, we have $U(P,f+g)\leq U(P,f) + U(P,g)$ and $L(P,f+g)\geq L(P,f) + L(P,g)$. Consequently,
\begin{align*}
\sup L(P,f) + \sup L (P,g) & \leq  \sup L(P, f+g) \\
& \leq \inf U(P,f+g) \\
& \leq \inf U(P,f) + \inf U(P,g)
\end{align*} 
Thus
$$\underline{\int^b_a}f\, + \, \underline{\int^b_a}g \, \leq \, \underline{\int^b_a}(f + g)\, \leq \overline{\int^b_a}(f+ g)\, \leq \,\overline{\int^b_a}f\, + \, \overline{\int^b_a}g$$
and since $f$ and $g$ are Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$, we have
$$\underline{\int^b_a}f  = \int^b_af = \overline{\int^b_a}f \qquad\text{and}\qquad \underline{\int^b_a}g = \int^b_a g = \overline{\int^b_a}g,$$
so that
$$\int^b_a f\, + \, \int^b_a g\, \leq \, \int^b_a(f + g)\, \leq \,\overline{\int^b_a}(f + g)\, \leq \, \int^b_af \, + \, \int^b_a g$$
This shows that all the inequalities are in fact equalities. Hence $f + g \in R[a,b]$ and 
$$\int^b_a (f + g) \, = \, \overline{\int^b_a}(f + g) \, = \, \int^b_a f \,  + \, \int^b_a g.$$
\end{proof}







\begin{thm}
Let $f,g\in R[a,b]$ and $f\leq g$ on $[a,b]$, then $$\int^b_a \leq \int^b_ag.$$
\end{thm}



\begin{proof}
For any partition $P$ of $[a,b]$ we have $L(P,f)\leq U(P,f) \leq U(P,g).$ This implies that
\begin{align*}
&\sup_P\{L(P,f) - U(P,g)\}  \leq 0\\
\implies \quad  & \sup_P\, L(P,f) - \inf\, U(P,g)  \leq 0\\
\implies \quad  &\int^b_a f\, - \, \int^b_a g  \leq 0\\
\implies \quad  & \int^b_af  \leq \int^b_a g
\end{align*} 
\end{proof}


\begin{thm}\label{thm:productint}
Suppose that $f,\, g\in R[a,b]$ then $fg\in R[a,b]$.
\end{thm}


\begin{proof}
Since $f, \, g\in R[a,b]$, they are bounded on $[a,b]$. Therefore, there exists $K> 0$ such that $|fg| = |f|\, |g|\, = \, K^2$ on $[a,b]$.

This implies that $fg$ is also bounded on $[a,b]$. Thus for any partition $P$ of $[a,b]$ both $U(P,fg)$ and $L(P,fg)$ exists and are bounded
We also have that for any $\varepsilon >0$ there exists a partition $P$ of $[a,b]$ such that $U(P,f) - L(P,f) <\varepsilon$ and $U(P,g) - L(P,g)<\varepsilon$.

Let $M_r(f)\, ,\, m_r(f)$ be bounds of $f$ on $[x_{r-1},x_r]$

$M_r(g)\, ,\, m_r(g)$ be bounds of $g$ on $[x_{r-1},x_r]$

$M_r(fg)\, ,\, m_r(fg)$ be bounds of $fg$ on $[x_{r-1},x_r]$

Then, for all $x',\, x''\in [x_{r - 1}, x_r]$ we have
\begin{align*}
|fg(x'') - fg(x')| & = |g(x'')\, f(x'') - f(x') + f(x')\,g(x'') - g(x')|\\
& \leq |g(x'')|\,
|f(x'') - f(x')| + |f(x')|\, |g(x'') - g(x')|\\
&\leq K\, |M_r(f) - m_r(f)| + K\, |M_r(g) - m_r(g)|
\end{align*} 
Thus $(M_r(fg) - m_r(fg))\leq K(M_r(f) - m_r(f)) + K(M_r(g) - m_r(g)).$ Hence 
\begin{align*}
\sum^n_{r=1}(M_r(fg) - m_r(fg))\, \Delta x_r &\leq K\sum^n_{r=1}(M_r(f) - m_r(f))\, \Delta x_r \\
&\qquad + K\sum^n_{r=1}(M_r(g) - m_r(g))\, \Delta x_r
\end{align*}
Thus $U(P,fg) - L(P,fg) < K\, \varepsilon + K\, \varepsilon = 2K\varepsilon\,.$ This implies that $fg\in R[a,b]$.
\end{proof}








\begin{thm}
Let $f,\, g\in R[a,b]$ and suppose that there exists $t>0$ such that $|g| > t$ on $[a,b]$, then $\frac{f}{g}\in R[a,b]$.
\end{thm}



\begin{proof}
The proof is similar to that of Theorem~\ref{thm:productint}, but in this case $\left|\frac{f}{g}\right|\leq \frac{K}{t}$ on $[a,b]$ and if $M_r\left(\frac{f}{g}\right)\, ,\, m_r\left(\frac{f}{g}\right)$ denotes the bounds of $\frac{f}{g}$ on $[x_{r-1}, x_r]$ then 
$$M_r\left(\frac{f}{g}\right) - m_r\left(\frac{f}{g}\right) \leq \dfrac{K}{t^2}\, (M_r(f) - m_r(f)) \, + \, \dfrac{K}{t^2}\, (M_r(g) - m_r(g)).$$
\end{proof}





\begin{thm}
If $f\in R[a,b]$ then $|f| \in R[a,b]$ and $\left|\displaystyle{\int^b_a f}\right|\, \leq \, \int^b_a|f|$.
\end{thm}




\begin{proof}
Since $f\in R[a,b],],$ there exists $K$ such that $|f| < K$ on $[a,b]$ and so $|f|$ is also bounded on $[a,b]$. Also given $\varepsilon > 0$ there exist a partition $P$ of $[a,b]$ such that $U(P,f) - L(P,f) < \varepsilon$.

Let $M_r(f)\, , \, m_r(f)\, ,\, M_r(|f|)$ and $m_r(|f|)$ so the bounds of $f$ and $|f|$ respectively on $[x_{r-1},x_r]$, then for all $x'\, , \, x''_r\in [x_{r-1},x_r]$ we have $||f(x'')| - |f(x')||  \leq |f(x'') - f(x')|$ and by taking supremum we have $M_r(|f|) - m_r(|f|) \leq M_r(f) - m_r(f)\,.$ Thus
\begin{align*}
U(P,|f|) - L(P,|f|) & = \sum^n_{r=1}(M_r(|f|) - m_r(|f|))\, \Delta x_r\\
& \leq \sum^n_{r=1}(M_r(f) - m_r(f))\, \Delta x_r\\
& = U(P,f) - L(L,f)\\
& < \varepsilon
\end{align*}
Hence $|f|\in R[a,b]$ since $|f|> f$ on $[a,b]$ we have
$$\left|\displaystyle{\sum^n_{r=1}M_r(f)\, \Delta x_r}\right|\leq \sum^n_{r=1}M_r \, |f|\, \Delta x_r$$
That is $|U(P,f)|\leq U(P,f)$ for all the partition $P\in P[a,b]$. Let $\|P\| \longrightarrow 0$  to get 
$$\left|\displaystyle{\int^b_a f}\right|\, \leq \, \int^b_a |f|$$
\end{proof}

















\begin{remark}
If $f\in R[a,b]$ and $M$ and $m$ are bounds of $f$ on $[a,b]$, then $$m\,(b - a)\leq \int^b_af \leq M\, (b-a)$$
\end{remark}



\begin{proof}
Since $m\leq f \leq M$ on $[a,b]$ , then for $f\in R[a,b]$ we have $$\int^b_a m \leq \int^b_a f \leq \int^b_a M$$

$$\implies\quad  m\,(b - a) \leq \int^b_a f\leq M\,(b-a)$$
These inequalities also follow from the fact that 
$$m\, (b - a) \leq L(P , f) \leq \int^b_a f \leq U(P , f) \leq M\, (b - a)$$
for any partition $P$.
\end{proof}








\begin{thm}[Mean Value Theorem of Integrals]
Suppose that $f$ is continuous and bounded in the closed and bounded interval $[a,b]$, then there exists a number $c$ with $a < c < b$ such that $$\int^b_a f(x)\,dx = (b-a)\, f(c)$$
\end{thm}




\begin{proof}
From the above \textit{Remark}; if $M$ and $m$ are bounds for $f$, then $$m\, (b -a )\leq \int^b_af(x)\, dx \leq M\, (b - a)$$

$$\implies\quad  m\leq \dfrac{1}{(b - a)}\, \int^b_af(x)\, dx \leq M$$
This shows that $\frac{1}{b - a}\,\int^b_a f(x)\, dx$ is a value between $m$ and $M$ of a continuous function on $[a,b]$. Therefore, by the intermediate value theorem for continuous functions on $[a,b]\, f$ takes this value at some point $c\in c[a,b]$. Thus $\frac{1}{b - a}\, \int^b_a f(x) \,dx = f(c)$ for some $c$ in $[a,b]$. Hence $$\int^b_af(x)\, dx = (b - a)\, f(c)$$
\end{proof}







\begin{thm}[Generalized Mean Value Theorem for Integrals]
Suppose that $f,\, g$ are continuous on $[a,b]$ and $g(x)\geq 0$ for all $x\in [a,b]$, then there exist a point $c\in (a,b)$ such that $$\int^b_af(x)\, g(x) \, dx = f(c)\, \int^b_ag(x)\, dx$$
\end{thm}





\begin{proof}
Let $m$ and $M$ be bounds of $f$ in $[a,b]$, then $m\leq f(x) \leq M$ for all $x\in [a,b]$. Since $g(x)\geq 0$ for all $x\in [a,b]$, we have $m\, g(x) \leq f(x)\, g(x) \leq M\, g(x)$ for all $x\in [a,b]$. This implies that 
$$m\, \int^b_ag(x)\, dx \leq \int^b_af(x)\, g(x)\, dx \leq M\, \int^b_ag(x)\,dx$$
Thus, there is a number $\mu$ with $m\leq \mu \leq M$ such that
$$\int^b_af(x)\, g(x)\, dx = \mu\, \int^b_ag(x)\, dx$$
But since $f$ is continuous on $[a,b]$, by the Intermediate Value Theorem there exists $c\in [a,b]$ such that $f(c) = \mu$. This implies that
$$\int^b_af(x)\, g(x)\, dx = f(c)\, \int^b_ag(x)\,dx\qquad \text{as required}$$
\end{proof}







\begin{exa}
Find a point $c$ in $[0,1]$ such that $\int^1_0 \frac{x}{1 + x^2}\,dx = 1$
\end{exa}



\begin{solution}
Let $f(x) = x$ and $g(x) = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}$. Then $f$ and $g$ satisfy the condition of the Generalized Mean Value Theorem. Therefore, there exists a point $c\in [0,1]$ such that 
\begin{align*}
\int^1_0x\,\left(\dfrac{1}{1 + x^2}\right)\, dx & = f(c)\, \int^1_0\dfrac{1}{1 + x^2}\, dx\\
& = c\, \int^1_0\dfrac{1}{1 + x^2}\, dx
\end{align*}
But $\int^1_0\frac{1}{1 + x^2}\, dx = \left.\tan^{-1}(x)\right|^1_0 = \tan^{-1}(1) - \tan^{-1}(0) = \frac{\pi}{4} - 0 = \frac{\pi}{4}$ and also


$\int^1_0\frac{x}{1 + x^2}\, dx = \left.\frac{1}{2}\, \ln (1 + x^2)\right|^1_0 = \frac{1}{2}\, \ln (2)$

Thus $\frac{1}{2}\, \ln(2) = \frac{\pi}{4}\, c\implies c = \frac{2}{\pi}\, \ln (2)$
\end{solution}


















\subsection{Fundamental Theorem of Calculus}

We now want to establish the fact that if $f$ is  a continuous function on $[a,b]$, then there exists a function $F$ on $[a,b]$ and that $F'(x) = f(x)$ for all $x\in [a,b]$. That is,every continuous function is the derivative of its integral.

Suppose that $f\in [a,b]$, define a function $F$ on $[a,b]$ by $f$ $$F(x)  = \int^x_af(t)\, dt,\quad  x\in [a,b]$$
Then $F$ is well defined since for each $x\in [a,b]\quad  f\in R[a,x]$ and as such $F(x)$ is uniquely defined on $[a,b]$.






\begin{thm}
Let $f\in R[a,b]$, then the function $F$ defined on $[a,b]$ by $$F(x) = \int^x_af(t)\, dt\quad \forall \, x\in [a,b]$$ is continuous on $[a,b]$. If $f$ is continuous at a point $c\in [a,b]$ then $F$ is differentiable at $c$ and $F'(c) = f(c)$.
\end{thm}



\begin{proof}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[]\textit{continuity}
If $f\in R[a,b]$, then $f$ is bounded on $[a,b]$ and so is $|f|$. Let $M$ be the upper bound of $|f|$ on $[a,b]$.Then given $\varepsilon > 0\, ,$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $0 < M\,\delta < \varepsilon$ and if $x\in [a,b],\quad x + h\in [a,b]$ and $|h| < \delta$, then
\begin{align*}
|F(x  + h) - F(x)| & = \left|\int^{x+h}_af(t)\, dt - \int^x_af(t)\,dt\right| \\
& = \left|\int^{x+h}_af(t)\,dt + \int^a_xf(t)\,dt\right|\\
& = \left|\displaystyle{\int^{x + h}_xf(t)\, dt}\right|\\ 
& \leq \int^{x + h}_x|f(t)| \, dt \\
& \leq M \, |h|  < M\, \delta < \varepsilon
\end{align*} 
Hence $F$ is continuous on $[a,b]$.


\item[]\textit{Differentiability at $c\in [a,b]$}
Since $f$ is continuous at $c\in [a,b]$ given $\varepsilon > 0$ there exists $\delta > 0$ such that $|x - c| < \delta   \, \implies\, |f(x) - f(c)| < \varepsilon$. Then for $s,\, t \in [a,b]$ with $s\neq t$ , $c-\delta < s\leq c \leq t < c + \delta$
\begin{align*}
\left|\dfrac{F(t) - F(s)}{t - s} - F(c)\right| & = \left|\dfrac{1}{t - s}\, \displaystyle{\int^t_s[f(x) - f(c)]\, dx}\right|\\
& \leq \frac{1}{t - s}\, \int^t_s|f(x) - f(c)|\,dx
 < \varepsilon
\end{align*}
Since $\varepsilon > 0$ is arbitrary, then $F'(c) = f(c)$
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}






\begin{thm}
\end{thm}

\textbf{First Fundamental Theorem of Calculus}
Let $f$ be a continuous function on the closed and bounded interval $[a,b]$, then if $$F(x) = \int^x_af(t)\, dt$$ then $F'(x) = f(x)$ for $x\in [a,b]$




\begin{proof}
For any fixed $x\in [a,b]$ chose $h\neq 0$ and $x + h \in [a,b]$. Then we have
\begin{align*}
F(x + h)  - F(x) & = \int^{x+h}_af(t)\,dt - \int^x_af(t)\, dt\\
& = \int^{x + h}_af(t)\, dt + \int^a_xf(t)\, dt\\
& = \int^{x + h}_xf(t)\, dt
\end{align*}
Since $f$ is continuous on the closed and bounded interval $[a,b]\, [x, x+h]$. Let $M$ be the maximum value of $f$ and $m$ its minimum value on $[x,x+h]$, then the intermediate value theorem implies that there exist points $t_1,\,t_2\in [x,x+h]$ such that $f(t_1) = m,\, f(t_2)= M$ and $m \leq f(t) \leq M$. Thus 
$$\int^{x  + h}_xm\, dt \leq \int^{x  + h}_xf(t)\, dt \leq \int^{x  + h}_xM\,dt .$$
so that $m\, h \leq \int^{x  + h}_x f(t)\, dt \leq M\, h$. Thus the number
\[
\theta = \frac{1}{h} \int^{x  + h}_x f(t)\, dt
\]
satisfies $m \leq \theta \leq M$. Again by the intermediate value theorem, there is a point $C(h)$ in $[x,x+h]$ such that $f(C(h)) = \theta$, so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ftcquot}
\dfrac{F(x + h) - F(x)}{h} = f(C(h)) .
\end{equation}
Since $x\leq C(h)\leq x + h$, we have $\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\, C(h) = x$. Since $f$ is continuous at $x$, the right side of \eqref{eq:ftcquot} has the limit $f(x)$ and the left side approaches $F'(x)$ as $h\rightarrow 0$.  So we get
$$F'(x) = \lim_{h\rightarrow 0} \, \dfrac{F(x + h) - F(x)}{h} = f(x)$$
if $h<0$, we take $[x + h, x]$ instead of $[x, x+h]$ and make necessary modification in the argument.
\end{proof}










\begin{thm}
\end{thm}

\textbf{Second Fundamental Theorem of Calculus}
If $f$ is  a continuous function on the closed bounded interval $[a,b]$ and if $\Phi' (x) = f(x)$ for every $x\in [a,b]$, then 
$$\int^b_af(x)\, dx = \Phi (b) - \Phi (a)$$




\begin{proof}
Let $F(x) = \int^{x}_a f(t)\, dt$. Since $f$ is continuous the first fundamental theorem implies that $F'(x) = f(x)$ for $a\leq x\leq b$. By the hypothesis $\Phi'(x) = f(x)$. Hence, we have $F(x) = \Phi (x) + c$ for $a\leq x \leq b$ and for a constant $c$. Therefore
\begin{align*}
F(b) - F(a) &  = [\Phi (b) + c] - [\Phi (a) + c]\\
& = \Phi (b) - \Phi (a)
\end{align*}
But $F(a) = \int^a_af(t)\,dt = 0$ by definition. Thus $F(b) = \Phi (b) - \Phi(a)$. Since $F(b) = \int^b_af(t)\, dt$ we have $\int^b_af(t)\, dt = \Phi (b) - \Phi(a)$ as required.
\end{proof}





\begin{remark}
If $\Phi' \neq 0$ on $[\alpha , \beta ]$, then $\phi$ is strictly monotonic on $[\alpha,\beta]$ so that the condition of strictly monotonic of $\Phi$ can be replaced by $\Phi'\neq 0$ on $[\alpha,\beta]$.
\end{remark}





\begin{remark}
The theorem still holds even if $\Phi' = 0$ for a finite number of times on $[\alpha, \beta]$. In that case $[\alpha, \beta]$ can be divided into a finite number of subintervals in each of which $\Phi$ is strictly monotonic.
\end{remark}








\subsection{Improper Integrals}
We want now to extend the definition of the Riemann integral $\int^b_af(x)\,dx$ to integrals over unbounded intervals as well as for functions which are unbounded at a point in the finite interval.



\begin{defn}
Let $f$ be bounded on the interval $[a,b]$. If either $b = \infty$ or $a = -\infty$, then the integral $\int^b_af(x)\,dx$  is called an improper integral of the first kind.

$$\text{e.g}\qquad \int^{\infty}_0\dfrac{1}{x^2}\, dx,\qquad  \int^0_{-\infty}e^x\,dx,\qquad  \int^{\infty}_0\sin x\, dx$$
\end{defn}







\begin{defn}
If $a$  and $b$ are real numbers and $f$ is unbounded at some point in $[a,b]$, then the integral $\int^b_af(x)\,dx$ is called an improper integral of the second kind 
$$\text{e.g}\qquad  \int^2_0\dfrac{x}{1 - x}\, \, dx,\qquad \int^1_0\dfrac{\sin x}{x^{3/2}}\,dx,\qquad \int^0_{-2}\dfrac{x^{1/2}}{1 + x}\, dx$$
\end{defn}



\begin{defn}
If the integral $\int^b_af(x)\,dx$ is defined over an unbounded interval and $f(x)$ is also unbounded at some point in the unbounded interval, then $\int^b_af(x)\,dx$ is said to be an improper integral of the third kind 
$$\text{e.g}\qquad \int^{\infty}_0\dfrac{1}{x^2 + x^{1/2}}\, dx$$
\end{defn}



\begin{defn}
Let $f$ be Riemann integrable for every $s\geq a$ and let $F(s) = \int^s_af(x)\,dx$ be its indefinite integral. Then $\int^{\infty}_af(x)\,dx$ is said to be convergent to $A$ if $F(s) \longrightarrow A$. Thus 
$$\int^{\infty}_af(x)\,dx = \lim_{s\rightarrow \infty}\, F(s) = A$$
If $\int^{\infty}_af(x)\,dx$ does not converge then $\int^{\infty}_af(x)\,dx$ is said to be divergent.
\end{defn}











\begin{exa}

$\int^{\infty}_1\frac{1}{x^2}\, dx$ is convergent, since
$$F(s) = \int^s_1 \frac{1}{x^2}\, dx = 1 - \frac{1}{s} \qquad\text{and}\qquad \lim_{s\rightarrow \infty}\left(1  - \frac{1}{s}\right) = 1 .$$

Hence $\int^{\infty}_1\frac{1}{x^2}\,\, dx = 1$

\
\end{exa}







\begin{exa}

$\int^{\infty}_1\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}\, dx$ is divergent, since
$$F(s)  = \int^s_1\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}\,dx = 2(\sqrt{s} - 1) \rightarrow \infty \quad\text{as } s \rightarrow \infty .$$
\end{exa}








\begin{exa}
consider the integral $\int^{\infty}_0e^{-\alpha\, x}\, dx$ where $\alpha$ is real number. Then $$F(s) = \int^s_0e^{-\alpha \, x}\, dx = \dfrac{1}{\alpha}\, (1  - e^{-\alpha\, s})$$
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i).] if $\alpha > 0$ then $\lim_{s \rightarrow \infty}\,\frac{1}{\alpha}\,(1  - e^{-\alpha\, s}) = \frac{1}{\alpha}$

\item[(ii).] if $\alpha < 0$ then $\lim_{s \rightarrow \infty}\,\frac{1}{\alpha}\,(1  - e^{-\alpha\, s}) = \infty$
\end{enumerate}

Therefore, the integral $\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\alpha\,x}\, dx$ converges if $\alpha > 0$ and diverges in $\alpha < 0$.
\end{exa}




\begin{exa}
Show that the integral $\int_a^{\infty}\frac{1}{x^p}\,dx\quad x>a > 0$
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i).] convergent for $p>1$
\item[(ii).] divergent for $p\leq 1$
\end{enumerate}
\end{exa}





\begin{solution}
The function $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^p}$ is continuous for any $x> a$ and 
$$F(s) = \int^s_a\dfrac{1}{x^p}\, \, dx = \dfrac{1}{1 - p}\left[\dfrac{1}{s^{p - 1}} \, -\, \dfrac{1}{a^{p-1}}\right]$$

Hence $\lim_{s\rightarrow \infty} F(s) = \frac{-1}{(1 - p)\, a^{p - 1}}$ if $p>1$ and $\lim_{s\rightarrow \infty} F(s) = \infty$ if $p<1$.


When $p = 1$ we have $F(s) = \int^s_a\frac{1}{x}\, dx = \ln(s) - \ln(a) = \ln \left(\frac{s}{a}\right)$ so that $\lim_{s\rightarrow \infty} F(s) = \infty$.


Hence the integral converges for $P>1$ and diverges for $p\leq 1$.
\end{solution}










\begin{thm}[Change of Variables]
Let $f\in R[a,b]$. Let $\Phi: [\alpha, \beta]\longrightarrow [a,b]$ be a differentiable, strictly monotone function on $[\alpha, \beta]$ such that $\Phi'\in R[\alpha,\beta]$. Then
$$\int^b_af(x)\, dx = \int^{\beta}_{\alpha}f(\Phi (t))\, \Phi'(t)\, dt$$
\end{thm}




\begin{proof}
We assume that $\Phi$ is increasing function on $[\alpha,\beta]$. Let $\Phi = \{\alpha = t_0,\, t_1,\, \dots ,\, t_n = \beta\}$ be the partition of $[\alpha,\beta]$ and $P  = \{ a = x_0,\, x_1,\, \dots ,\, x_n = b\}$ be the corresponding partition of $[a,b]$ such that $x_i= \Phi (t_i)\, , \, i = 0,\, 1,\, 2\, \dots ,\, n$. Then the Mean Value Theorem implies that
$$\Delta x_r = x_r - x_{r - 1} = \Phi (t_r) - \Phi (t_{r - 1}) = \Phi (\eta_r)\, \Delta t_r$$
where $\eta_r \in [t_{r-1},t_r]\, \, r = 1,\, 2, \, \dots , n$.

Let $\zeta_r = \Phi (\eta_r),\quad  r = 1,\, 2,\, \dots , n$.
\begin{equation}\label{eq:substsum}
\sum f(\zeta_r) \, \Delta x_r = \sum f(\Phi (\eta_r))\, \Phi'(\eta_r)\, \Delta t_r .
\end{equation}
Since $\Phi$ is differentiable on $[\alpha,\beta]$ it is uniformly continuous on $[\alpha,\beta]$ and consequently $|\Phi|\longrightarrow 0$ as $|P|\longrightarrow 0$.

Now let $|P|\longrightarrow 0$ so that $\sum f(\zeta_r)\, \Delta x_r \, \longrightarrow\, \, \int^b_af(x)\, dx$ and

 $\sum f(\Phi (\eta_r))\, \Phi'(\eta_r)\, \Delta t_r \longrightarrow \int^{\beta}_{\alpha} f[\Phi(t)]\, \Phi'(t)\, dt$

Equation \eqref{eq:substsum} then gives $\int^b_a f(x)\, dx \, = \, \int^{\beta}_{\alpha} f(\Phi (t))\, \Phi'(t)\, dt$


Theorem also holds for monotone decreasing function with minor adjustment in the above argument.
\end{proof}











\begin{thm}

Suppose that the integrals $\int^{\infty}_af(x)\, dx$ and $\int^{\infty}_ag(x)\, dx$ are both convergent. Then $\int^{\infty}_a[f(x)\pm g(x)]\, dx$ is convergent and $$\int^{\infty}_a[f(x) \pm g(x)]\, dx   = \int^{\infty}_af(x)\, dx + \int^{\infty}_ag(x)\, dx .$$
\end{thm}



\begin{proof}
Since $\int^{\infty}_af(x)\, dx$ and $\int^{\infty}_ag(x)\, dx$ are both convergent, the limits $\lim_{s\rightarrow \infty} F(s)$ and $\lim_{s\rightarrow \infty} G(s)$ exist. Let $\lim_{s\rightarrow \infty} F(s) = A$ and $\lim_{s\rightarrow \infty} G(s) = B.$  Then 
\begin{align*}
\int^{\infty}_a[f(x)\pm g(x)]\, dx  & = \lim_{s\rightarrow \infty}\, \int^s_a[f(x)\pm g(x)]\, dx\\
& =\lim_{s\rightarrow \infty}\, \left[\int^s_af(x)\, dx \, \pm \, \int^s_ag(x)\, dx\right]\\
& =\lim_{s\rightarrow \infty}\, \int^s_af(x)\, dx \, \pm \,\lim_{s\rightarrow \infty}\, \int^s_ag(x)\, dx\\
& = A\, \pm \, B
\end{align*}
This proves that $\int^{\infty}_a[f(x)\,\pm\,g(x)]\, dx$ is convergent and $$\displaystyle{\int^{\infty}_a[f(x)\,\pm\,g(x)]\, dx = \int^{\infty}_af(x)\, dx + \int^{\infty}_ag(x)\, dx}$$
\end{proof}
















\subsection{Riemann-Stieljes Integral}


Let $f,\, \alpha$ be functions defined on the interval $[a,b]$. We shall assume through out the discussion that $\alpha$  is an increasing function and $f$ is bounded. Let $P:\, a = x_0 < x_1 < \dots < x_n = b$ be a partition of $[a,b]$. We define  $M_k(f) = \sup\{f(x):\, x_{k -1} < x < x_k\}\quad ,  \quad  m_k = \inf\{f(x):\, x_{k-1} < x < x_k\}$. Note that since $f$ is bounded, $M_k(f)$ and $m_k(f)$ are finite. Let $t_k$ be a point in $[x_{k-1},x_k]\quad  k = 1,\, 2,\, \dots ,\, n$.

Define $\Delta \alpha_k  = \alpha (x_k) - \alpha (x_{k - 1}),\quad  k = 1,\, 2,\, \dots , n$

The upper sum associated with the partition $P, \, f,\, \alpha$ is defined as $U(P, f, \alpha) = \sum^n_{k =1}\, M_k(f)\, \Delta \alpha_k$

Similarly, the lower sum is $L(P, f, \alpha) = \sum^n_{k = 1}\, m_k(f)\, \Delta \alpha_k$.


The Riemann-Stieljes sum is $S(P, f, \alpha) = \sum^n_{k = 1}\, f(t_,)\, \Delta \alpha_k$






\begin{defn}
If $f:\, [a,b]\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ is bounded function such that $U(P, f, \alpha)$ and $L(P, f, \alpha)$ approach a common finite limit as $\|P\|\longrightarrow 0$, then we say that $f$ is Riemann-Stieljes integrable with respect to $\alpha$ or that the Riemann-Stieljes integral of $f$ with respect to $\alpha$ exists. The integral is the denoted by $\int^b_af(x)\, d\alpha (x)\quad \text{or}\quad  \int^b_af\, d\alpha$.

If $\alpha (x) = \alpha$ then $\int^b_af\,d\alpha$ is the used Riemann integral of $f$ denoted by $\int^b_af(x)\, dx$.
\end{defn}

\textbf{Pre-requisites:} Introduction to Real Analysis


\textbf{Course content}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Topology of the Real Line}
Open and closed subsets of $\mathbb{R}$: neighbourhoods, interior points, exterior and boundary points, limit points of a set: Bolzano-Weistrass theorem: Cantor intersection theorem; compactness, the Heine-Borel theorem; compact sets and connected sets.


\item \textbf{Continuous functions}

Continuity at a point and on a set; properties of continuous functions on closed intervals; uniform continuity; the intermediate value theorem; preservation of connectedness and of compactness under continuous mappings; the maximum and minimum value theorem; uniform convergence; step function approximation; functions of bounded variation.

\item \textbf{Functions of bounded variation}
Algebraic properties of functions of bounded variation; functions of bounded variation as a difference of two increasing functions; continuity of functions of bounded variation; absolute continuity of functions of bounded variation.


\item \textbf{Differentiable functions}
Differentiability at a point and on a set; differentiability and continuity; some theorems on differentiation; Rolle's theorem; Mean-value and generalized mean value theorem with applications; Taylor's theorem with applications.


\item \textbf{The Riemann integral}
Partition of an interval; refinements of a partition; Riemann sums; Riemann integral and the integrability criterion of Riemann classes of integrable functions; properties of the Riemann integral; integral Calculus; the first mean value theorem for integrals; the Riemann Stieljes integral; improper integrals.
\end{enumerate}









\subsection{Practice Problems}

\begin{problem}
Let $f$ be Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$ and define
$F(x) = \int_a^{x} f(t)\,dt$. Show that $F$ is continuous on $[a,b]$, and that
$F'(c)=f(c)$ at every point $c$ where $f$ is continuous.
\end{problem}
\begin{solution}
\subhead{Continuity}
$f$ is bounded, say $|f|\leq M$. For $x<y$ in $[a,b]$,
$$|F(y)-F(x)| = \left|\int_x^{y}f(t)\,dt\right| \leq M(y-x),$$
so $F$ is Lipschitz with constant $M$, hence uniformly continuous. Continuity of
$F$ needs no continuity of $f$ at all --- only boundedness.

\subhead{Differentiability where $f$ is continuous}
Let $f$ be continuous at $c$ and let $\varepsilon>0$. Choose $\delta>0$ with
$|f(t)-f(c)|<\varepsilon$ for $|t-c|<\delta$. Then for
$0<|h|<\delta$,
$$\left|\frac{F(c+h)-F(c)}{h} - f(c)\right|
= \left|\frac{1}{h}\int_c^{c+h}[f(t)-f(c)]dt\right|
\leq \frac{1}{|h|}\cdot|h|\varepsilon = \varepsilon .$$
Hence $F'(c) = f(c)$.

The two halves differ in what they need, and the difference is the content of
the theorem: integration always smooths, producing a Lipschitz function from a
merely bounded one, but it recovers the integrand as a derivative only where the
integrand was already continuous.
\end{solution}
